Echinococcus granulosus* causes cystic echinococcosis and is the form most frequently encountered.

The adult *Echinococcus granulosus* tapeworm (2 to 7 mm long) resides in the small intestine of the definitive host. Gravid proglottids release eggs that are passed in the feces and are immediately infectious. After ingestion by a suitable intermediate host, eggs hatch in the small intestine and release six- hooked oncospheres that penetrate the intestinal wall and migrate through the circulatory system into various organs, especially the liver and lungs. In these organs, the oncosphere develops into a thick- walled hydatid cyst that enlarges gradually, producing protoscolices and daughter cysts that fill the cyst interior. The definitive host becomes infected by ingesting the cyst- containing organs of the infected intermediate host. After ingestion, the protoscolices evaginate, attach to the intestinal mucosa, and develop into adult stages in 32 to 80 days. Humans are aberrant intermediate hosts and become infected by ingesting eggs. Oncospheres are released in the intestine and hydatid cysts develop in a variety of organs including bones.

*Echinococcus granulosus* occurs practically worldwide, and more frequently in rural, grazing areas where dogs ingest organs from infected animals. In the United States most infections are diagnosed in immigrants from counties where cystic echinococcosis is endemic.

Clinical presentation. *Echinococcus granulosus* infections of bones often remain asymptomatic for years before the cysts grow large enough to cause symptoms in the affected organs. Primary bone involvement occurs in 0.5% of all patients with hydatid cysts. There is only one case of hydatid cyst reported in the hand involving the scaphoid (Fig. 3.51). The patient had symptoms of pain and mild wrist stiffness for only a month. Radiographic examination, MRI, and serologic studies were nondiagnostic, and bone biopsy established the diagnosis. Curettage and bone graft were curative.

![[Fig.3.51.png]]
> **Fig. 3.51** Primary hydatid cyst of the scaphoid. A, There is a well-demarcated cystic structure at the proximal pole of the left scaphoid on the preoperative anteroposterior radiograph. B, A 10-mm × 5-mm hyperintense mass is seen on T2-weighted coronal cross section of the scaphoid on preoperative MRI. C, Histopathology examination demonstrated hydatid cyst scolex, which is the head-like part of the tapeworm. There was inflammatory cell infiltration in the surrounding fibrous tissue (hematoxylin-eosin stain; magnification ×200). (Images from Bulut M, Tosun HB, Simsek BC, Karakurt L: Primary hydatid cyst of the scaphoid: case report, J Hand Surg Am 37[5]:1051–1053, 2012.)

#### Roundworm Infections

**Gnathostomiasis.** Gnathostomiasis is a subcutaneous infection caused by a roundworm (nematode), *Gnathostoma spinigerum.* It is a chronic, cutaneous, creeping (migrating) larval infection. It is common in Thailand, Southeast Asia, and Japan, where people eat uncooked fish or raw pork. In Thailand and China, it is known as “tua- chid” (painful tumor) and Changchiang edema, respectively. The definitive host for this organism belongs to the cat and dog family. Aquatic lower animals like frogs, snakes, and fishes are the second intermediate host. Consumption of uncooked, raw meat or contaminated fish appears to be the likely cause of infection in humans. Daengsvang et al. alluded to five cases of gnathostomiasis occurring in the fingers in Southeast Asia.Subhedar et al. and Mukherjee et al. independently reported one case each in India. Multiple migrating episodes of swelling and pain can occur in the hand, foot, shoulder, and face over several years as larva migrate subcutaneously, in contrast to the constant swelling of cutaneous larva migrans (CLM) caused by hookworm. *Gnathostoma* *spinigerum* intermittently migrates between superficial and deeper structures. Eosinophilic count is typically high because eosinophils are specifically effective against helminthic parasites. A skin test and a serum precipitin test are useful in confirming the clinical diagnosis. Exploration, recognition, and removal of the larva from the infected part of the hand is curative. Albendazole and ivermectin are two antiparasitic medications that have been used successfully in patients with gnathostomiasis.

**Onchocerciasis.** Onchocerciasis is a rare parasitic infection of the soft tissues caused by a roundworm (nematode), *Onchocerca volvulus.*

Onchocerciasis of the flexor tendons of finger, wrist, carpal tunnel, and forearm have been reported in North America. Eosinophilia is pathognomonic of parasitic infestations. Synovectomy is curative. Avermectin is highly efficient against roundworm infections that affect millions in tropics and subtropics, but it is not necessary if all the parasites are removed with the infected tissue. Onchocerciasis can also cause skin disease with intense itching, rashes, or nodules under the skin.

**Hookwormrelated cutaneous larva migrans (HrCLM).** Hookwormrelated cutaneous larva migrans (HrCLM) (also called creeping eruption, creeping verminous dermatitis, sandworm eruption, or plumber’s itch) is a skin disease caused by infection with the larvae of animal hookworms, particularly those that infect cats and dogs. The infecting species of this disease include *Ancylostoma braziliense* and *Ancylostoma caninum.* HrCLM in American travelers to Caribbean countries and to Central and South America was reviewed by Patrick Hochedez and Eric Caumes in 1980. CLM in the upper extremity was reported by Belsole and Fenske in 1980. This tropical dermatosis is not sufficiently well known by Western physicians, and this can delay diagnosis and effective treatment. Indeed, misdiagnosis or inappropriate treatment affects 22% to 58% of the travelers with CLM. In one case report, the time lag between the onset of disease and the diagnosis was 22 months.

Epidemiology and geographic distribution. HrCLM is one of the leading causes of dermatologic disorders observed in travelers. The hookworms responsible for CLM are distributed worldwide, but infection is more frequent in the tropical and subtropical countries of Southeast Asia, Africa, South America, the Caribbean, and the southeastern part of the United States. HrCLM is rare in temperate countries, but a few autochthonous cases have been reported from Europe, North America, and New Zealand. A risk factor for infection is failure to wear protective footwear while walking on a beach frequented by dogs and cats.

Pathophysiology. HrCLM is caused by the penetration of the skin by cat, dog, or other mammal nematode larvae. The adult hookworms live in animal intestines, and their eggs are spread on the soil during defecation. Egg hatching and larva survival are facilitated by moist grounds like beaches. After 2 months in the soil, larvae become infectious and acquire the ability to penetrate the host skin. The infection is usually acquired via contact with soil or sand contaminated with feces of infected cats or dogs, which explains why bare feet, legs, thighs, buttocks, hands, and elbows the predominant sites of penetration by the parasite larvae. During skin penetration, the secretion of hyaluronidase by hookworm larvae facilitates passage through the epidermis and dermis. Without appropriate treatment, the larva dies and is resorbed within weeks or months of invasion.

Clinical presentation. CLM is easy to diagnose because a creeping eruption is often clearly visualized as it migrates at a rate of 1 to 2 cm per day. In contrast to the intermittent CLM caused by *Gnathostoma spinigerum* , HrCLM appears as a constant eruption. The striking symptom of HrCLM is intense pruritus localized at the site of the eruption. It is reported in 98% to 100% of patients. The incubation period of HrCLM is usually a few days and rarely goes beyond 1 month. The mean time of onset after return from foreign travel ranged from 5 to 16 days. However, some extremely long incubation periods have been reported, lasting for 4 to 7 months. Creeping eruption is an erythematous, linear, or serpiginous track that is approximately 3 mm wide and may be up to 10 cm in length. It may affect the palm or a finger. The creeping track may extend a few millimeters to a few centimeters daily. The mean number of lesions per person varies from one to three. Edematous and vesiculobullous lesions may occur along the course of the larva. Local swelling is reported in 6% to 17% of patients and vesiculobullous lesions in 4% to 40%. Without any treatment, the eruption usually lasts between 2 and 8 weeks before it dies, but it may be longer. In one case report, active disease was reported for almost 2 years. Secondary bacterial infection of the involved skin area occurs in up to 8% of cases.

Diagnosis. HrCLM is usually a clinical diagnosis based on the typical clinical presentation in the context of recent travel to a tropical or a subtropical country and beach exposure. Clinical characteristics of the creeping trail (length, width, speed of migration, location, duration) easily help differentiate HrCLM from other causes of creeping dermatitis. Total blood count could detect eosinophilia in 20% of the patients.

Treatment. Campbell of Drew University, Madison, New Jersey, and Omura of Japan were awarded the 2015 Nobel Prize for Medicine “for their discoveries concerning a novel therapy (avermectins) against infections caused by roundworm parasites” that infect millions all over the tropical and subtropical world. Oral ivermectin and albendazole are the firstline treatments. Taken in a single dose, ivermectin is well tolerated and highly efficacious, with cure rates of 94% to 100% in all but one of the largest series. After effective treatment, the pruritus has been shown to disappear much sooner than the eruption (on average 7.2 days earlier). After ivermectin treatment, symptoms disappear within 1 week: 3 days (1 to 20) for pruritus and 7 days (1 to 30) for creeping dermatitis. In case of initial failure or relapse, cure can be obtained with one or two repeated doses of ivermectin. Side effects related to ivermectin in this use are rare and not systemic. Albendazole is also effective and well tolerated in a dose of 400 to 800 mg once a day for 3 days. For lightweight children or when oral ivermectin and albendazole are contraindicated, application of a 10% albendazole ointment, twice a day for 10 days, is a safe and effective alternative treatment.

Prevention. When lying on tropical beaches, hands and posterior side of elbows become infected, and lying on bare sand should be avoided on beaches frequented by cats and dogs.

## INFECTIONS (INFESTATIONS) CAUSED BY INSECTS

Discussion of infections caused by insects can be found in the online version of the chapter at Elsevier eBooks for Practicing Clinicians(ExpertConsult.com).

## OCCUPATIONAL INFECTIONS

Barber’s interdigital pilonidal sinus, shearer’s disease, milker’s interdigital granuloma, and slaughterer’s interdigital granuloma are foreignbody occupational hand infections caused by penetration of human or animal hair into skin. Anthrax, *M. marinum,* tuberculous paronychia, herpetic paronychia, prosector’s wart, milker’s nodule, human orf, and viral warts are also infectious occupational diseases of the hand and are described in detail in the bacterial, tuberculous, and viral infection sections of this chapter.

### Barber’s Interdigital Pilonidal Sinus

#### Etiology and Epidemiology

Barber’s interdigital pilonidal sinus of the hand is a foreign- body hair granuloma. The term *pilonidal* is derived from the Latin terms *pilos,* which means “hair,” and *nidus,* which means “nest.” Interdigital pilonidal sinus in barbers was first described in 1942 as a foreign- body reaction or a granuloma of the customer’s hair implanted in the barber’s interdigital skin. Since then, cases have been described as hair- bearing sinus, barber’s interdigital pilonidal sinus, barber’s disease, and interdigital sinus. Interdigital pilonidal sinus is a disease of male barbers and hairdressers only. It has never been reported in female hairdressers. It rarely affects the pulp of a finger. The condition is not a congenital cyst because hair roots are not seen in the sinus or the cyst, and the hairs are not immature, curly, or fine like those found in a congenital pilonidal cyst.

#### Clinical Findings and Diagnosis

Clinically, a pit or sinus develops in the interdigital web space dorsally. The sharp, snipped hair- ends enter the soft skin of the digital webs when barbers or hairdressers use their fingers to comb the

#### Flatworm Infections

**Filariasis.** The lymphatic filariasis is caused by mosquito- borne filarial nematodes *Wucheria bancrofti* , *Brugia malayi,* and * B. timori.* These parasites are classified as nematodes (roundworms) of the family Filarioidea. They have more affinity toward the lymphatic system and cause lymphatic filariasis. In contrast, the nematodes like Onchocerca volvulus and Loa loa have more affinity to skin and soft tissue and cause filariasis of the skin. Bancroftian filariasis caused by *W. bancrofti* accounts to 90% of lymphatic filariasis.

Epidemiology and clinical manifestation. Bancroftian filariasis is endemic in Western Pacific islands, Madagascar, the Caribbean region, and sub- Saharan Africa. Sporadic incidence is noted in Southeast Asia, India, and South America. *Brugia* spp. is more commonly seen in Southeast Asia, and *B. timori* is endemic in Indonesia. The clinical presentation differs according to the phase, which can be acute or chronic. The acute phase is characterized by the microfilaremia and is associated with eosinophilia. Constitutional symptoms are common. The chronic phase is characterized by skin and subcutaneous changes due to microfilaria- associated inflammation and fibrosis resulting in elephantiasis, commonly in the lower extremities and rarely in the upper extremities.

Diagnosis. Identification of microfilaria in the peripheral smear, done from a centrifuged nocturnal blood sample drawn from the patient, is diagnostic. The challenge of diethylcarbamazine (DEC) prior to the drawing of nocturnal blood sample increases the yield of the diagnosis. DEC gets concentrated in the lymphatics and the microfilaria trying to escape from its toxicity migrate into the bloodstream. In the early and late stages of filariasis, the microfilaria may not be present in the peripheral blood. Circulating filarial antigen test is easy to perform, does not have diurnal variation, and has high sensitivity and specificity and is performed with just a pin prick. The major disadvantage of the antigen test is the cost and availability of the test in developing countries where the disease is endemic.

Treatment. Oral DEC 100 mg thrice daily for 3 weeks is the first line of treatment for the condition. Albendazole (400 mg) alone twice per year, ivermectin (200 mcg/kg) with albendazole (400 mg), and DEC citrate (6 mg/kg) and albendazole (400 mg) in combination have been recommended by the WHO for treatment and also for mass administration to eliminate filariasis.

A case of Bancroftian filariasis in a 32- year- old female presenting with a small subcutaneous swelling at the lateral aspect of the upper arm demonstrates ease of early diagnosis and treatment. There was a complaint of a painless swelling over the lateral aspect of her left upper arm for 5 months. On examination, there was a 2 × 2.5 cm nontender, immobile swelling, which was firm in consistency and adherent to the skin. It was not warm to touch. There were two to three lymph nodes, nontender, in the left axilla. FNAC was done, with aspiration of about 0.3 mL of clear fluid. The smear showed presence of thin, slender colorless thread- like larvae with blunt head and tail tip free of nuclei. These were identified as *W. bancrofti* (see eFig. 3.2). Another case of midarm swelling as a rare presentation of filariasis from India was recently presented by Oza et al.

![[eFig.3.2.png]]
> **eFig. 3.2** Fine-needle aspiration cytology (FNAC) of about 0.3 mL of clear fluid. The smear showed presence of thin, slender colorless thread-like larvae with blunt head and tail tip free of nuclei. These were identified as W. bancrofti. (Giemsa stain with ×100 oil magnification.)

**Loa loa ﬁlariasis (loiasis).** Loiasis, or African eye worm, is caused by infection with the filarial nematode Loa loa. The parasite is transmitted by deerflies in the *Chrysops* genus, which breed in tropical rainforests in West and Central Africa. When the fly bites, it injects adult microfilaria into the subcutaneous tissue. The adult microfilaria reside and multiply here. The swelling caused by the adult worm is known as Calabar swelling (eFig. 3.3).

![[eFig.3.3.png]]
> **eFig. 3.3** Loa loa filariasis (loiasis). The localized subcutaneous edema (so-called Calabar swelling) is thought to result from toxic allergic reaction from the death of microfilaria in the subcutaneous tissue. (Courtesy of Joseph Kamgno. From Kamgno J, Klion AD: Loiasis. In Ryan ET, Hill DR, Solomon T, Aronson NE, Endy TP (eds): Hunter’s Tropical Medicine and Emerging Infectious Diseases, 10th ed, Philadelphia, Elsevier, 2020: 859–863.)

Clinical manifestations. The illness is characterized by Calabar swellings, which are 3 to 10 cm in dimension and may or may not be erythematous. These swellings are caused by the immune allergic reaction to the larva toxins. When these swellings are chronic, they have layers of fibrous connective tissue surrounding them and may be painful. The acute swellings usually last for around 3 days and are itchy. These worms can migrate to conjunctiva and may cause blindness. Usually the fly bite precedes the illness. Symptoms can occur as early as 4 months or delayed years after the fly bite.

Diagnosis. The ideal diagnostic procedure is a microscopic examination of thick peripheral blood smear to identify microfilaria by Giemsa staining. The microfilaria can be identified in urine and saliva samples also. The presence of the worm in the Calabar swelling and the worm extruding from the swelling are also diagnostic. Although nonspecific, patients with the infection also have eosinophilia and high IgE levels.

Treatment. The treatment is usually with antihelminthic drugs like DEC, albendazole, and ivermectin as monotherapy or in combination.

Attempts at surgical removal of the worms are useful to alleviate the local symptoms and the anxiety associated with extruding worms from the swellings.

The Caribbean islands and Central and South America are among the most popular travel destinations for Americans. And some of these visitors will come home harboring unwelcome skin infestations—infestations that may eventually bring them to a hand surgeon’s attention. According to surveillance conducted by the CDC and the International Society for Travel Medicine, the two most common tropical skin infestations brought back by U.S. travelers to the North Americas are myiasis and tungiasis.

Myiasis (fly maggots) skin infestation commonly occurs in Central and South America, Africa, and Asia. Tungiasis is common in Africa and South America, especially Brazil. Mites (scabies), fly larvae (maggots), and sand flea *Tunga penetrans* may infest the skin and HrCLM may infect the subcutaneous tissue of hand and upper extremities.

### Scabies

First described in 1687, with the organism responsible identified in the 18th century, this intensely pruritic disease was named from the Latin word for scratch “ *Scabere.* ” The discovery marked scabies as the first human disease with a known cause.

#### Etiology and Epidemiology

The five individual countries with greatest scabies burden are Indonesia, China, Timor- Leste, Vanuatu, and Fiji. Scabies is not an infection but an infestation of the epidermis and dermis by a microscopic eightlegged mite, the *Sarcoptes scabiei.* Each mite is less than half a millimeter long, and under a magnifying glass, mites can be seen on skin as black dots. Typically, an affected host is infested by only about 10 to 12 adult mites. After mating, the male dies. The female mite burrows into the epidermis and dermis of the skin where she lives, lays up to three eggs each day for her 1- to 2- month lifetime, and dies.

Development from egg to adult scabies mite takes 10 to 21 days. They are highly contagious and can be spread by close, skin- to- skin contact. Institutions (e.g., nursing homes, extended- care facilities, and prisons) are often sites of scabies outbreaks. People who have a decreased immune system sometimes develop a more severe form of scabies known as Norwegian scabies, which refers to a particularly severe form of scabies that is also known as crusted scabies. Crusted scabies is called Norwegian scabies because the condition was first described in Norway in the mid- 19th century.

#### Clinical Findings

Scabies presents with severe itch and a papular rash, with a predilection for the hands, feet, and genitalial folds. The distribution may be more widespread in infants and older people. Secondary bacterial infection is also common in patients with scabies. Any area of the body can be affected, but the interdigital regions of fingers and folds of wrist, elbow, and armpit are the more common sites in the upper extremity. Rash and intense itching are cardinal symptoms. If it is the first time a person has had scabies, he or she may not develop symptoms for up to 6 weeks. During this period, those infected are contagious despite not having symptoms. Scabies can spread easily among people who have close physical contact with an infected person; the longer the skin- toskin contact, the more likely the spread.

#### Diagnosis

Clinical examination remains the mainstay of diagnosis. Each mite is less than half a millimeter long, and this may make them difficult to spot. A magnifying glass (dermatoscope) is useful. To the naked eye, they may look like tiny black dots on the skin. From a skin scraping, a microscope can identify mites, eggs, or fecal matter.

#### Treatment

Treatment is required to cure scabies and prevent spread to others. Treatment consists of killing the mites, symptom control, and decontamination. In the United States, permethrin cream 5% (Elimite) is used to treat scabies by killing the mites. Treatment should be considered for all those who have had skin- to- skin contact with an infected individual. Vigorous scratching can lead to a bacterial infection requiring treatment with an antibiotic. Although the rash may not be present all over the body, patients are usually instructed to apply the cream to all areas of the body except the face. If infants are being treated, directions may include application of the cream to the scalp and head. A second application of medication may also be needed in some cases. Attention to the area under the fingernails is necessary. If the scabicide medication is used correctly, it usually kills the mites relatively quickly.

A particular danger of scabies is that these lesions often predispose to the development of secondary infections, as with *Staphylococcus* bacteria. Like typical scabies, Norwegian scabies is treated with topical permethrin cream and the oral medication ivermectin (Stromectol). Treatment of crusted scabies can require oral medications along with multiple applications of scabicide cream. After effective treatment, no new rashes will develop. Although the mites are killed, itching can still persist for a few weeks.

#### Prevention

Prevention includes maintaining personal hygiene, changing clothes and bed linens daily, and regular daily baths. Home care involves cleaning and removing the mites from areas of the home that the infected person may have come into contact with. This includes boiling clothes and bed linens in hot water, drying them in the sun and ironing them, thorough scrubbing of skin with soap and water, thorough drying of skin after bathing, and treating all close contacts. This helps prevent reinfection and spreading of the infection to others. The infected person should avoid skin- to- skin contact with others until after treatment. With the correct use of hygiene, proper home care, and medications for the patient and contacts, scabies can be effectively treated and prevented.

### Myiasis

Myiasis is derived from the Greek word *myia,* meaning fly. The term was first introduced by Hope in 1840 and refers to the infestation of human skin and subcutaneous tissue with fly larvae (maggots), which feed on the host’s dead or living tissue. Flies in the order *Diptera* cause myiasis, and the distribution of human myiasis is worldwide. Cutaneous myiasis is a temporary parasitic infestation of the skin. Larvae of many flies from various geographic areas can cause myiasis: In Central and South America, human cutaneous myiasis is mainly caused by the larvae of *Dermatobia hominis* ; in Africa, by the larvae of *Cordylobia anthropophaga* ; and in the northern hemisphere (North America, Europe, Africa, and Asia), by the larvae of *Hypoderma* spp. There is greater abundance in socioeconomically poor regions of tropical and subtropical countries. In countries where it is not endemic, myiasis is an important traveler’s condition, where it represents the fourth most common travel- associated skin disease. Increasing trends toward immigration and global travel to tropical and subtropical areas will likely increase the frequency of encounters with such parasitic cutaneous infestations in North America and Western countries.

Cutaneous myiasis can be divided into three main clinical manifestations: furuncular myiasis, wound (traumatic) myiasis, and creeping (migratory) myiasis. The flies that produce a furuncular myiasis include *Dermatobia hominis, Cordylobia anthropophaga, Wohlfahrtia vigil,* and the *Cuterebra* species. Flies that cause wound myiasis include screwworm flies such as *Cochliomyia hominivorax, Chrysomya bezziana,* and *Wohlfahrtia magnifica. Gasterophilus* and *Hypoderma* are two flies that produce a creeping myiasis.

Furuncular myiasis is the most common. Larvae (maggots) grow inside the host subcutaneous tissue while feeding on it as parasites. Infestation of skin and subcutaneous tissue causes a furuncle at the site of infestation. The flies that produce furuncular myiasis include *Cordylobia anthropophaga, Dermatobia hominis, Wohlfahrtia vigil,* and *Cuterebra species.* The tumbu fly, *Cordylobia anthropophaga* (also known as the botfly, gadfly, heel fly, mango fly, putzi fly, tumbu fly, warble fly, and skin maggot fly), is endemic to tropical Africa, especially East Africa and Central Africa. The tumbu fly and its larva were first described in Senegal, Africa, in 1862. The human botfly, *Dermatobia hominis,* is indigenous to Central and South America only. Seven cases of *Dermatobia hominis* myiasis have been reported in the United States since 1999. All the patients with confirmed *Dermatobia hominis* myiasis had recently visited Central or South America. Both African and South American flies cause furuncles indistinguishable from each other.

The fly commonly infects humans by laying its eggs on wet clothes that are left outside to dry. The eggs hatch in 1 to 3 days and the larvae can survive without a host for up to 15 days. When the clothes are worn, the larvae breach the skin and burrow into the subcutaneous tissue. They do not kill the host; thus they are true parasites. Clinical presentation of furuncular myiasis begins as a “bite” that looks like a normal mosquito or flea bite, and then slowly develops into a 2 to 3 mm very itchy, sore, infected boil. The main distinguishing factor is that there is constantly a hole in the center, which is the maggot’s breathing hole. One may feel the larvae wiggling inside the lesion.There may be serous or purulent discharge from the hole. Typically, the larvae penetrate the skin on the posterior aspect of the limbs, back, or buttocks. Myiasis is often misdiagnosed in the United States because it is rare, and its symptoms are not specific. Clues that myiasis may be present include recent travel to an endemic area, one or more nonhealing lesions on the skin, itchiness, movement under the skin or pain, discharge from a central punctum (tiny hole), or a small, white structure protruding from the lesion.

Treatment consists of removing the larva from the subcutaneous tissue. The easiest and most effective way to remove botfly larvae is to apply petroleum jelly over the location, which prevents air from reaching the larva, suffocating it. It can then be removed with tweezers safely after a day. In some patients, the larva may be popped out by applying pressure around the wound. In some cases, injection of lidocaine under the lesion creates pressure in the cavity in which the larva resides and forces it out. It is important not to break the larva to prevent granulomatous inflammatory host reaction. The most conventional way of removing the larva is with a simple surgical procedure that includes local anesthesia. Using a scalpel to cut a slit to enlarge the wound, the larva can be taken out. After any of these procedures, antibiotics are given to prevent infection. The wound should heal in 1 to 2 weeks with little or no scarring. Oral use of ivermectin, an antiparasitic medicine, has proved to be an effective and noninvasive treatment that leads to the spontaneous emigration of the larva. After extraction of the larva, the patient’s arm pain and swelling are rapidly resolved. Travelers to East and Central Africa and to Central and South America need to take preventive measures, including applying insect repellent and wearing well- ironed, protective clothing.

Wound myiasis occurs when fly larvae infest open wounds. It has been a serious complication of war wounds in tropical countries and is sometimes seen in neglected wounds in most parts of the world. Predisposing factors include socioeconomically poor conditions and extremes of age, neglect, mental disability, psychiatric illness, alcoholism, diabetes, and vascular occlusive disease. Wound myiasis affects the skin with a previous breach in the skin, and larvae may consume both dead and living tissue in the wound. Untreated, digital myiasis may result in necrosis and gangrene of entire digit with cellulitis extending into proximal hand (eFig. 3.4), requiring amputation and removal of entire population of larvae. One such case was caused by *Cochliomyia hominivorax* in Brazil. *Chryomya bezzinia* and *Wohlfahrtia* may also cause wound myiasis.

![[eFig.3.4.png]]
> **eFig. 3.4** A and B, Digital wound myiasis (maggot infestation) by fly (Cochliomyia hominivorax) larva. Cutaneous myiasis is the infestation of the skin by larvae of the order Diptera, the common two-winged fly. A 41-year-old female with history of psychiatric disorders immigrated to Portugal from Brazil, after she suffered a knife injury to the second finger of the left hand and did not seek immediate medical care; instead, a handkerchief was used to control the bleeding and cover the wound for 6 months. A strong odor emitted from the finger. There was a large area of finger necrosis and self-amputation, associated with cellulitis extending from the finger’s base to the hand. With the naked eye, one could observe the live larvae infesting the healthy tissue in pocket-like holes under the skin. Metacarpophalangeal disarticulation and mechanical removal of 132 live larvae was done. Morphologic study determined the larvae to be C. hominivorax (Diptera: Calliphoridae)—New World screworm (NWS)—larvae. The NWS is endemic in Central and South America. (From Durão C, Barros A, Campos P: A rare case of digital myiasis, J Infect Public Health 10[6]:886–887, 2017.)

Creeping myiasis is a rare cutaneous myiasis characterized by infestation of humans by the larvae of horse or cattle botflies. Horse botfly larvae, after penetration of the skin, form tunnels in the lower layers of the epidermis, where they can migrate for up to several months, causing serpentine, erythematous lesions with visible creeping eruptions and intense reactive pruritus. In contrast, cattle botfly larvae penetrate deeper into the subcutaneous tissue, producing more painful, erythematous lesions, which usually resolve locally after several hours or days, when the larvae move on to infest another area. Gasterophilus larvae cause serpentine, erythematous, raised lesions, which advance at one end and gradually fade at the other end. Clinically, diagnosis may be made by placing a small amount of mineral oil over the lesion that makes the black transverse bands of spine of larvae visible. Ultrasonography is useful in localizing larvae before they are removed. Once larvae are localized, they may be removed with a needle through a tiny incision. *Hypoderma lineatum* larvae of the Himalayan region leave a yellow patch as they migrate to infest another area. They are best removed through a larger cruciform incision.

### Tungiasis

Tungiasis is caused by infestation of skin epidermis with the sand flea, *T. penetrans,* also called the jigger flea.

#### Etiology and Epidemiology

Tungiasis is a zoonosis that affects a broad range of domestic and peridomestic animals, such as dogs, cats, pigs, and rats. Where humans live in close contact with these animals, the risk for infection is high. This ectoparasitosis is endemic in economically depressed communities in Africa and South America, especially Brazil. In 1184 inhabitants of a poor neighborhood in northeastern Brazil, 33.6% were found to have tungiasis (95% confidence interval = 30.9% to 36.4%). Of this population, 6% presented lesions at locations other than the feet, with the hands being the most common ectopic site (5.5%). The other site in the upper extremity was the elbow. This is an imported disease in developed countries. Numerous case reports describe travelers who have returned from the tropics with a mild disease. In current literature, this ectoparasitosis is mainly mentioned as an exotic nuisance for the casual traveler in South America and sub- Saharan Africa. Tourists seldom have more than one lesion and usually seek medical advice within 1 week of flea penetration, resulting in the extraction or excision of the parasite at an early stage. However, in northeast Brazil, patients may carry up to 145 sand fleas in all stages of development.

Various mechanisms exist by which embedded fleas could induce pathologic alterations of the skin in an early stage of development. Acute inflammation with erythema, edema, pain, and itching is due to tissue damage induced by a metabolically highly active and continuously enlarging parasite. As with other blood- sucking insects, *T. penetrans* releases proteolytic enzymes during penetration and growth, causing an inflammatory response of the skin. The immune response of the host might contribute to the intense inflammation observed soon after penetration.

As the lesion develops, bacterial superinfection almost inevitably occurs. During penetration, the flea breaks up the stratum corneum, allowing bacterial microcolonies on the skin surface to spread. In addition, pathogenic microorganisms on the outer surface of the flea may be actively carried into the epidermis.

#### Clinical Findings

In economically depressed areas where poor housing, social neglect, and the practice of sleeping in soil is prevelant, there is a high risk of tungiasis transmission. With a length of <1 mm, *Tunga penetrans* is the smallest known flea species. The female jigger flea penetrates into the skin of its host. Within 10 days, the flea increases its volume by a factor of approximately 2000, finally reaching the size of a pea. Through its hindquarters, which serve for breathing, defecating, and expulsing eggs, the flea remains in contact with oxygen in the air, leaving a sore (240 to 500 m) in the skin; the sore is an entry point for pathogenic microorganisms, leading to secondary infection. The preferred localization for jiggers is the periungual region of the toes and fingers. These lesions show signs of intense acute and chronic inflammation. Superinfection of the lesions leads to pustule formation, suppuration, and ulceration. In a study from Brazil, debilitating sequelae occurred, such as loss of nails with difficulty using the hands and feet.

Having reviewed 14 cases of tungiasis imported to the United States, Sansui et al. reported that the patients showed only one or two lesions and that, except for itching and local pain, no clinical pathology was observed. In contrast, indigenous populations and deployed military personnel frequently suffer from severe disease, characterized by deep ulcerations and tissue necrosis, leading to denudation of bones. Tungiasis has also been associated with lethal tetanus in nonvaccinated persons. In a study in São Paulo, Brazil, tungiasis was identified as the cause of tetanus in 10% of cases.

Once burrowed in the skin of its host, the flea becomes hypertrophic, produces and releases eggs, and eventually dies. Microbiologically, the embedded flea behaves as a foreign body with a continuously enlarging surface. The parasite remains buried in the epidermis. When the skin’s surface is breached to underlying tissue layers, translocation of the normal microflora occurs, leading to a bacterial infection.

As the lesion itches immediately after the flea penetrates, patients usually start to scratch, which, in turn, promotes the entry of bacteria through the persistent sore in the epidermis. In fact, microabscesses are observed in histologic sections of lesions only 2 days after penetration.

In many patients, bacterial superinfection was also the result of an inappropriate manipulation of lesions with nonsterile instruments by the patient or caregiver. The remarkable desquamation of the skin observed around late- stage lesions has its histopathologic correlates in hyperkeratosis and parakeratosis of the stratum corneum.

A 6- year- old girl of an unmarried and illiterate mother in Brazil had 96 lesions. Of the 96, a total of 86 were located on the feet. Ten lesions were found on the index finger of the right hand and impeded the girl from gripping (eFig. 3.5). Lesions were surrounded by severe erythema and edema. Pustules and suppurations were frequent. The family lived in a hut with sandy soil.

![[eFig.3.5.png]]
> **eFig. 3.5** Untreated tungiasis lesions are prone to become infected, and clinicians should be aware of its hand localization. Second finger of the right hand with abscesses, acute erythema, and edema. (From Feldmeier H, Eisele M, Sabóia- Moura RC, Heukelbach J:Severe tungiasis in underprivileged communities: case series from Brazil, Emerg Infect Dis J 9[8]:949–955, 2003.)

#### Diagnosis

A definitive diagnosis depends on documentation of the flea itself either through a mineral oil preparation or a skin biopsy of the suspected nodule. A histologic examination will reveal the flea’s body in the intraepidermal cavity lined with eosinophilic cuticle. The cavity may also contain the round or oval eggs and hollow ring- like components of the flea’s respiratory and digestive tract. Inflammation is usually noted in the underlying dermis. Sometimes, a fluid will ooze from the central opening of the nodule and *T. penetrans* eggs may be seen under the microscope.

#### Treatment

Treatment involves a topical antiparasitic agent such as ivermectin, metrifonate, or thiabendazole and removal of the flea with sterile forceps or needles. But there is a promising new treatment concept: topical dimethicone, or polydimethylsiloxane. Studies have shown that following application of dimethicone, roughly 80% to 90% of sand fleas are dead within 7 days. It is nontoxic and has a purely physical mechanism of action, so resistance is unlikely.

The flea can be removed from its cavity surgically. The orifice needs to be enlarged, and the entire flea nodule should be curetted out. Following extraction of the flea, thoroughly cleanse and cover the remaining crater with a topical antibiotic cream to prevent secondary infection. A course of oral antibiotics may be instituted if secondary infection is suspected. Ensure that tetanus prophylaxis is up to date.

customer’s hair. Constant rubbing of the fingers against each other exacerbates the problem. Once inside the web space, the hairs cause a foreign- body reaction and sinus formation. As more hairs enter the web space through the sinus, a cyst can form. In time, the sinus or the cyst becomes infected and an abscess forms that intermittently discharges purulent fluid with pieces of hair. Histologically, the lesion reproduces the features of a nonspecific inflammatory granuloma.

Several loose and easily removable hairs of different lengths, colors, and textures generally protrude from the sinus. They have sharp, pointed ends and are consistently thick, stiff, and straight, essentially the male type. This may explain why the condition occurs in men’s hairdressers. Women’s hair is generally soft and thin, and the ends are more likely to bend rather than penetrate. Rarely is more than one interdigital web space affected. The order of predilection is the third web space followed by the second and fourth. The first web space has been reported to be involved only once.

#### Treatment

Excision of the lesion and primary wound closure results in a less obvious scar than incision and drainage.

### Shearer’s Disease

A similar and related condition to barber’s interdigital sinus occurs in the interdigital spaces of sheep shearers and is caused by wool. Small tufts of wool may protrude from the sinus. Symptoms are made worse after shearing lambs because newer, finer wool tufts enter the open sinus with ease. It can result in a recurrent subungual pilonidal sinus and can cause chronic osteomyelitis of the subjacent phalanx. The foreign- body hair granuloma is a well- recognized occupational hazard in dog groomers in the United Kingdom as well.

### Milker’s Interdigital Granuloma

The hairs of cattle, barbed at an angle, can penetrate skin of milker’s hands and migrate slowly and deeply under the skin and cause a foreign- body reaction. Milker’s interdigital granuloma is a painful, red, granulating, discharging, vegetating lesion found in the hands of milkers. It can vary from pea- size to nut- size, and the surface may be irregular and riddled with sinuses. The second and third interdigital spaces of the dominant hand are the usual location. Treatment is removal of all hair and control of the inflammatory process. A persistent granuloma should be excised.

### Slaughterman’s Interdigital Granuloma

Slaughterhouse employees can develop a pilonidal sinus in the interdigital space from hair of the animals they slaughter. The lesion persists with conservative treatment until it is completely excised.

## CONCLUSION

In the coming years, physicians will continue to see four types of chronic infections of the hand.

- We will improve our diagnostic ability in identifying chronic infec

tions of the hand. As an example, atypical mycobacterial hand infections were not recognized until the 1960s. These are, however, easy to diagnose as long as biopsy, staining, serology, cultures, and sequencing methods are routinely used for diagnosis.

- More elusive diseases may be identified to arise from organisms that

were not previously suspected. Herpes virus was not recognized as a cause of KS until 1986, when detection and quantitation of specific DNA and RNA base sequences in clinical specimens became available.

- Physicians will see chronic infections that are caused by newly rec

ognized or mutated organisms. Fungal infections are on the rise and recently identified fungal infections are reported more frequently. Many of these fungal infections respond inadequately to currently available antifungal antibiotics.

- Finally, chronic infections in developed countries will continue

to increase as worldwide travel and immigration bring older and exotic organisms into new habitats. Cases of leishmaniasis trickle into Texas from Mexico and into all parts of the world from those who vacation in Costa Rica.Awareness of the characteristics of chronic hand infections, and vigilant use of biopsy and culture techniques, should be able to reveal and control such infections in the upper extremity.
